BFCL v4 · 20 checkpoints head-to-head · 20 transcripts rendered in full

Reason vs No-Reason

Two SFT runs over the same base model, the same corpus, the same ten epochs — one trained to emit reasoning traces before its tool calls, one trained to emit the calls alone. This page puts them in a single table, cell against cell, and then opens twenty complete conversations so you can read what the numbers are made of.

The table settles it quickly. Across 60 comparable cells — ten epochs × six metrics — the no-reason run wins 58, ties one, and loses one. There is no epoch at which reasoning is the better checkpoint, and no metric on which it closes.

−2.90 · mean of 10 epochs

85.60vs83.08
−2.52

71.92vs68.95
−2.97

85.16vs76.08
−9.08 · widest gap

2.23vs0.78
−1.45 · both at the floor

12.99vs7.96
−5.03 · nearly halved

  • reasoning (86 steps/epoch)

Part one

What BFCL v4 actually measures

The Berkeley Function Calling Leaderboard tests whether a model can pick the right function and fill its arguments correctly. Version 4 keeps the single-turn and multi-turn categories from v1–v3 and adds agentic ones on top, so running v4 gives the earlier signal too. We ran 20 of its 22 scored categories, 5,017 generated entries per model.

Category groupEntriesWhat it asks of the modelHow it is scored
Non-live simple / multiple / parallel1,150Pick one function, or several, or several at once, from a hand-written catalogueAST match
Live (user-contributed)2,251Same, but on real function schemas donated by users — messier, more realisticAST match
Irrelevance / relevance1,140Recognise when no offered function fits, and abstain — or when one doesAbstention correctness
Multi-turn (4 categories)800Hold a conversation against a live stateful API — a filesystem, a vehicle, a trading bot — across several user turns, including turns with a missing function or parameterFinal API state + response
Memory (kv / vector / rec-sum)465Read and write to a memory backend across a session and use what it storedFinal state
Web search (excluded)200Multi-hop search and page-reading via a live search APIAnswer match

The three scoring mechanisms differ in kind. AST match parses the emitted call and compares function name and arguments against accepted answers — it never executes anything. Multi-turn and memory are state-based: the benchmark runs the calls against a real Python object and compares the final state of that object to ground truth, so a conversation that reaches the right end state passes even by a different route, and one that gets every call right but leaves the state wrong fails. Part three shows exactly this happening.

The weighting is where the headline number comes from

BFCL v4's Overall Acc is not an average of categories. It is a fixed weighting that puts 70% of the score on multi-turn and agentic work:

10101030 · multi-turn40 · agentic
  • Non-live
  • Live
  • Irrelevance
Overall = 0.10·NonLive + 0.10·Live + 0.10·Irrelevance + 0.30·MultiTurn + 0.40·Agentic Agentic = mean(Memory, WebSearch)

We verified this against the published CSV: it reproduces every one of our 22 rows to the second decimal.

Our Overall Acc figures are not comparable to the public leaderboard. We skipped web_search — the SerpAPI key on hand is free-tier, 250 searches against a category of multi-hop tasks needing several searches each, across 22 models. A missing category is scored as 0, not dropped, so web search being absent halves the agentic term and costs every model up to 20 points of Overall.

Comparisons within this page remain exact — every model is missing the same category. Only the absolute Overall numbers are depressed.

Part two

The head-to-head

Two SFT runs over Qwen3-4B-Base on a ToolACE-derived corpus: one rendering assistant turns as bare tool calls (no-reason, 64 steps/epoch), one interleaving reasoning traces (reasoning, 86 steps/epoch). Ten epoch checkpoints each, all 5,017 entries, no partial evaluation. Served through vLLM 0.8.5 in prompting mode with Qwen3's own tool template, which renders byte-identically to the template the models were trained under.

Every cell is a percentage. The winner of each pair is shaded — blue where no-reason wins, orange where reasoning does. The point of the table is the colour, not the digits: it is blue almost everywhere.

Δ -2.90 -2.52 -2.97 -9.08 -1.45 -5.03

Δ is reasoning minus no-reason, on the ten-epoch mean. Negative everywhere.

The single orange cell is epoch 2, non-live AST — 85.88 against 83.17. Epoch 1 multi-turn is an exact tie at 1.25. Every one of the other 58 cells goes to no-reason. Twenty independently trained checkpoints agreeing this consistently is not sampling noise.

One caveat on causality, which the table cannot settle: the two corpora differ in supervised-token density as well as in content — 36.6% of tokens supervised in the reasoning run against 14.1% in the no-reason run. This compares two training recipes, not reasoning-versus-no-reasoning in isolation.

Part three

Where both runs sit

The head-to-head only says which fine-tune is better. These five rows say whether either one matters. The base model appears twice — once as BFCL scored it, once re-scored from the same generations with a lenient parser that accepts a well-formed call object without the <tool_call> wrapper.

ModelOverallNon-LiveLiveIrrel.Multi-TurnMemory
Qwen3-4B-Baseuntrained, strict parse10.990.000.00100.000.004.95
Qwen3-4B-Baseuntrained, lenient re-parse12.3358.6740.4917.921.381.08
SFT no-reason e7best of the no-reason run28.0185.9670.4787.412.6314.19
SFT reasoning e3best of the reasoning run25.1384.7570.0277.201.008.17
Qwen3-4B Instructofficial reference38.5187.2381.7979.0731.7520.86

Base's 0.00 is a parser artifact. 399 of 400 simple_python outputs contain a well-formed {"name": …, "arguments": …} object with the right function and arguments — but 0 of 5,017 generations wrap it in the tags the parser requires. Re-scored leniently, with no new inference and no new sampling, the untrained base calls functions at 58.67% non-live and 40.49% live. A fidelity check confirms the rewrite invents nothing: 45,826 extracted calls, zero whose function name is absent from the raw output.

So the real gain from fine-tuning on single-turn is +28.5 points, not +87. And base's 100.00% irrelevance detection collapses to 17.92% the moment its calls parse — it was never abstaining, nothing parsed. Read the other way, teaching the model when not to call (17.92 → 85.16 mean for no-reason) is the largest genuine gain either fine-tune produced.

Part four

Twenty conversations, rendered whole

Scores compress a conversation to one bit. These do not. Five multi-turn tasks were drawn at random — seeded, from a fixed shuffle across all four multi-turn categories: two from base, one each from miss_func, miss_param and long_context. Every task is then replayed for four models: the untrained Base, the official Instruct reference, and the best checkpoint from each SFT run — no-reason epoch 7 and reasoning epoch 3.

All four models saw the identical system prompt and the identical scripted user turns, so the tabs are directly comparable: switch between them and only the model's own behaviour changes. Each card opens with that shared system prompt and the starting API state, then the conversation — user turns, the assistant's reasoning trace where it emitted one, its tool calls, and the real values the API returned.

None of the twenty passed. That is the expected shape of a random draw here, not a selection: at their measured pass rates a clean sweep of five failures is the most likely outcome for Instruct (about 15% likely) and near-certain for the two fine-tunes, which pass roughly 1 multi-turn task in 40. Nothing was filtered — the draw is what it is, and the failure banner on each tab names the exact scoring verdict.

How to read a card

ElementWhat it is
System promptThe full # Tools block BFCL builds — every function schema serialised flat, then the <tool_call> protocol instruction. 19–28 KB per task. Identical across all four tabs.
Initial API stateThe world before turn 1: the filesystem tree, the account balances, the vehicle's fuel level. Ground truth is a state comparison against this object after the last turn.
Turn n → step kA turn is one scripted user message. Within it the model may take several steps, each a generation followed by the tool results it triggered. A turn ends when the model emits no call.
reasoning traceCollapsed by default. Present for Instruct and for the reasoning-run checkpoint; the no-reason checkpoint emits none by construction.
tool responseWhat the API actually returned — including its error strings. These are the moments that decide the task, and the ones the fine-tunes ignore.
handler_logThe harness narrating itself. Empty response from the model. Proceed to next turn. means the model answered in prose instead of calling anything, and the turn was closed out.
multi_turn_miss_param_108

multi-turn · missing parameter

MessageAPI + TradingBot · 30 functions in scope

System prompt 19,006 characters — the identical prompt all four models received

Sure, I can assist you with that. To reverse a stock transaction, you typically need to cancel the order or execute a reverse transaction if the platform supports it. Since you didn't provide the order ID or specifics of the transaction, I can guide you on the general steps to reverse a stock transaction:

1. **Identify the Order ID**: Locate the order ID of the transaction you wish to reverse. This is crucial as it helps in identifying the specific transaction to be reversed.

2. **Contact Your Broker or Trading Platform**: Reach out to the support team of your broker or trading platform. Explain the situation and provide them with the order ID.

3. **Provide Necessary Documentation**: You may need to provide additional documentation or justification for reversing the transaction, especially if it's a large transaction or if there's a specific reason for the reversal.

4. **Execute the Reversal**: Once approved, execute the reversal. This might involve placing a new order to sell the stock or requesting a cancellation of the existing order, depending on the platform's capabilities.

5. **Confirm the Reversal**: Ensure that the reversal is processed correctly and confirm the details with your broker or trading platform.

If you need assistance to find the order ID or any other specific details, please let me know how you'd like to proceed!

step 0
assistant
reasoning trace · 120 words
The user is asking about the stocks they've been tracking, i.e. their watchlist. The request is straightforward: retrieve the current watchlist so they can analyze the stocks and plan their next moves. Looking at the available tools, `get_watchlist` is the direct match — it returns the list of stock symbols currently in the user's watchlist. The other tools don't fit: `add_to_watchlist` and `remove_stock_from_watchlist` are for modifying the watchlist, not reading it; `get_available_stocks` returns stocks in a sector rather than the user's own watchlist; `get_stock_info` gives details about a single stock, not a list; and the rest (account info, orders, messages, trading auth) are unrelated. The function takes no required parameters, so the call goes out with an empty argument object.

step 0
assistant
reasoning trace · 136 words
The user wants the current price of 'Quasar Ltd.' — a stock lookup. The request is straightforward: identify the company, fetch its price, and return it. No further computation or chaining is implied. The trading system is the natural fit here, and `get_stock_info` is the tool that returns price, percent change, volume, and moving averages for a given symbol. The other trading tools don't match: `get_available_stocks` lists symbols in a sector rather than a named company, `get_symbol_by_name` goes the other way (company to symbol), and the rest (watchlist, orders, account info, login/logout) are unrelated. The only required parameter is the symbol, and the user named the company directly, so I'll pass 'QUASAR' as the symbol. The threshold and date-range tools are for price-change notifications and transaction history, not a single price lookup, so they don't apply.

Part five

What the transcripts show

Reading 1 · open the Base tab first

The untrained model is not answering — it is writing the rest of the conversation

Base's transcripts are unmistakable. It produces a correct call object, then keeps going: it invents the user's next question, answers that too, invents another, and runs until the generator is cut off. The delimiters it emits between those imagined turns decode as the stray fø you can see in the text — a base model reaching for a chat token it was never trained to place.

The numbers behind that impression, across all 800 multi-turn tasks: 85.3% of Base's generations run to the 1,000-token cap, against 0.6% for the no-reason checkpoint and 6.3% for Instruct. And 0 of 3,336 contain a <tool_call> tag — the single fact behind Base's 0.00 across four categories, and the reason its re-parsed score is 58.67% rather than zero.

Read the step counts too. Instruct takes 8,060 generations to work through the same 800 tasks; the no-reason checkpoint takes 5,334, and Base only 3,336. Fewer steps is not efficiency here — it is turns ending early because nothing callable came out.

Reading 2

Prose is a hard stop, and both fine-tunes reach for it

The harness ends a turn the moment a generation contains no parseable call. So an assistant message that says "the file is not present in the current directory — verify its location" is not a partial step towards the answer; it is the end of the turn, and the next user message arrives against unchanged state. Across the suite, 38–51% of fine-tune turns contain no tool call at all.

The pattern is visible in every card: a plausible opening batch of calls, a precise error from the API, and then an explanation addressed to the user rather than a correction addressed to the tool.

Reading 3

The models do not recover from errors they can read

On the 625 turns across the suite that hit a tool error, the fine-tunes issued any follow-up call only 16–20% of the time. The errors are not cryptic — {"error": "mv: cannot move 'final_report.pdf': No such file or directory"} names the file and the reason. What is missing is the move that follows: cd into the directory that holds it. Across 200 filesystem tasks, cd was called 11 times.

Reading 4

Reasoning traces make the failure longer, not different

Open the same card on the reasoning tab and then the no-reason tab. The reasoning checkpoint deliberates at length, then emits the same call the other model emitted directly — or, more often, deliberates and emits nothing. The traces do not surface the missing cd; they narrate around the error and conclude with advice.

They do cost context. The reasoning checkpoint hit the served context window on 26 multi-turn entries against the no-reason checkpoint's 16; the untrained base, which emits no traces, hit it zero times. Instruct — verbose by design — hit it 25 times, including one of the five tasks below, where its transcript is a context-length error rather than a conversation.

Reading 5

Failing at the end state is not the same as failing at every call

Several cards carry the verdict multi_turn:instance_state_mismatch — the model made sensible calls throughout and still left the API in the wrong state. Multi-turn is graded on that final object, not on the calls, which is why a transcript can read well and score zero. It is also why the multi-turn column is not a gentler version of the non-live column: the two measure different things, and the fine-tunes are strong at one and absent at the other.

Part six

How to read these numbers

Three caveats, in the order they affect conclusions.

Caveat 1

Overall Acc is depressed by roughly 20 points for every model

web_search is absent and counts as 0 against 40% agentic weight. Comparisons within this page are exact; do not put these Overall figures next to the public leaderboard.

Caveat 2

Tool schemas reach the model in a shape it never trained on

BFCL serialises tool docs flat — {"name": …, "parameters": {"type": "dict"}} — while all 67,288 tool definitions in both training corpora used the OpenAI-wrapped form with "type": "object". You can see the flat form in any card's system prompt. We kept BFCL's rendering deliberately: it is what every official Qwen3 entry gets, so the numbers stay leaderboard-shaped. It means single-turn scores may understate both runs slightly — and it affects both equally, so the head-to-head is unaffected.

Caveat 3

19 entries could not run at all

18 multi_turn_long_context entries exceeded the context limit and one crashed on a KeyError. That is a harness ceiling, not a model failure, and it caps that category at 91% of its entries for every model equally. One of the five sampled tasks hits it on the Instruct tab.

What follows

The corpus is the thing to change

The head-to-head answers which recipe to keep — no-reason, at 58 of 60 cells — but the transcripts say that choice is worth about three points of Overall against a gap of ten times that. Single-turn is solved at epoch 1 of either run and does not improve with more training. Multi-turn is at the floor for both, at every epoch, while a same-size model that saw multi-turn data reaches 31.75%.

The concrete check is cheap: count how many conversations in the corpus have more than one assistant turn, and how many contain a tool response carrying an error that the next assistant turn has to act on. If the second number is near zero, that is the finding — and the fix is data, not epochs, and not reasoning traces.

20 SFT checkpoints + 2 references × 5,017 entries · BFCL v4 (20 of 22 scored categories) · vLLM 0.8.5, bfloat16, temperature 0.001 · 8×A100-40GB · scores from score/data_overall.csv, no partial evaluation · transcripts read verbatim from result/*/multi_turn/, sampled with a fixed seed
